# On the Spiritual Word and the Perfection of the Soul

From those things that belong to them in perfection. Just as the birth that comes from those things which are opposite to them [is] the change of thought. And he mixed it by the hand of all of them, because in the time of the perfection of the soul, she speaks from God. **Prayer** is in the likeness of the **Word from God**—the **Word from God**—which is seen upon the altar <note>original: ܡܐܕܢܐ (*ma'dna*); this likely refers to a spiritual table or altar where the Divine Word is "served" to the soul</note>. So that there might be a descent <note>lit: a stroke or a landing</note> from that which is of the visible things; a spiritual Word that illuminates within the one who received it. 

In this way, you also see from all of them that from which her soul gushes forth, like a spiritual catch <note>original: ܨܝܼܕܐ (*tsayda*); literally a "hunt" or "provision," here referring to the spiritual insights "captured" by the soul</note> that she demonstrates by her words, from that which is surrendered to those who approach her in perfection. Or she seeks to gush forth from the one who hears the Word; and the Word that mixes—which is of a spiritual nature—in the soul that received it. 

Just as the spiritual catch shows in the likeness of the proclamation of the Word, which is from heaven, to that which approaches with diligence; as it illuminates in divinity to that which received it, and that which is from that joy to those who received Him. This becomes for them like a provision of perfection. And also, as a provision that you see others also becoming. 

So that this provision might be a fountain of the likeness of the soul. Or a seeking, as we said, from the one who received it, so that it might show to the hearer that they have received Him. Just as it is from that which gushes forth from that which is like a provision—which gushes from the one who received it—as one who is valiant. To those who received Him, as it shows a provision that gushes forth from that which belongs to those who received Him. 

Just as it is from that which gushes forth from that which is like a provision—which gushes from that which she received. And it shows that even this which you see from all of them, as it illuminates in the one who received it, from that which is like a provision that you see. Just as the Word which mixes in the one who received it, from that which is in perfection. 

Because in the time of the perfection of the soul, she speaks from God in the likeness of the **Word from God**—the **Word from God**—which is seen upon the altar, so that there might be a descent from those who received Him. It shows that even this which you see, as it illuminates in the one who received it, is from that which belongs to the soul. Or a seeking that illuminates in the one who received it, as you also see from all of them.

ܡܿܢ <margin>Who/From</margin>

<note>Glossary of key Syriac terms used in this passage:
Prayer (Slotha): The communicative act of the soul.
Soul (Naphsha): The seat of spiritual experience.
Word (Meltha): Used here both for the "Logos" (Christ) and the inspired speech of the soul.
God (Alaha): The source of the Word.
Perfection (Shalmutha): The state of spiritual maturity required for this divine dialogue.
Provision/Catch (Tsayda): The "harvest" of spiritual knowledge.
Illuminates (Manhra): The action of the Divine Word upon the human soul.</note>

<summary>This page contains a Syriac mystical treatise on how the soul, upon reaching spiritual perfection, enters into a dialogue with God. It describes the "Word" as a divine illumination that "mixes" with the soul and provides spiritual nourishment or a "catch" of divine wisdom.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discourse on the soul's perfection but shifts into a philosophical investigation of fasting (asceticism). While the previous page was in Syriac, this page is written in Garshuni—Arabic language written using the Syriac alphabet. This transition is common in Near Eastern manuscripts where philosophical concepts often blended Syriac Christian and Arabic Islamic/Neoplatonic traditions.</meta>

# On the Nature of the Soul and the Purpose of Fasting

The soul said regarding all this: she is not—by her nature—divine among the divines through fasting <note>original: ܨܝܐܡ (*siyam*); the Arabic word for fasting, used here in an ascetic, philosophical sense</note>. Along with this is the **fasting of the ears**, just as she teaches us. A fasting person is not found [to truly fast] unless she finds her [the soul's] essence. In her particularity, fasting is not found [inherently], for if it were found there, it would not be of her nature; she has existed without fasting as well. If she were fasting by her very nature, she would never break her fast at all.

But by being overcome by a power other than herself, fasting has come to be found in her. Initially, there was in her a state of non-fasting in a complete form. She is transparent and free of impurity; in her original form, she was not fasting.

The proof is correct: + She is a purifier, and all of her is receptive to purity without the essence of filth or impurity <note>original: ܓܘܗܪ (*jawhar*); essence or substance</note>. Like the **form of light**, she is not made impure by the lower realms; even if there were impurity near her, she remains pure from every wickedness. Likewise, the transparent form is not a "fasting form" so long as it remains in the majority [the physical state] until it is distinguished. 

We establish that through the cycles, in the majority of her states, the pure form finds fasting within itself. This is a form that is not impure, and its mastery is that it becomes ascetic <note>original: ܡܬܩܫܦܗܿ (*mutaqashifah*); practicing 'qushafa' or harsh asceticism</note> through fasting, becoming purer than any other form, for it is radiant. 

And this concludes this limit. There is also another proof:

A truthful scholar <note>likely referring to a Neoplatonic commentator</note> said that all of it is distinguished from what belongs to him. In her there was a state of solitude <note>original: ܐܢܦܪܐܕ (*infirad*); solitary isolation or singleness</note>, and for that reason she became, by nature, removed from all imagery—which has been separated from every nature either by choice or by necessity. For solitude in fasting, as **Plato** <note>original: ܐܦܠܛܘܢ (*Aflaton*); the Greek philosopher Plato was highly influential in Eastern mystical traditions regarding the soul's separation from the body</note> has said, does not exist as "fasting" within the Divine Nature in the assembly. This is because the Divine is not in need, nor is there need in any other thing found in the simple, unique nature. As for where the fasting of Plato leads, it is perfection.

This is also a proof of all of it: that by fasting on that day, the soul is purer than any other and more eloquent than it. 

Or consider **blameworthy fasting**. As for blameworthy fasting, if it is done other than for the sake of the Perfect One, it becomes merely part of the assembly and the [temporary] relief found therein. Just as she has come to accept the internal [spirit], so fasting in all its matters should not be something that overpowers life, but rather the "required overcoming." She is, by nature, beloved. As for the branching out of her fasting, all of it was within her from him.

<summary>This page presents a philosophical argument on whether fasting is natural to the soul, concluding that while not inherent, fasting serves as a purifying tool to return the soul to its radiant, "Platonic" state of solitude.</summary>
<keywords>soul, fasting (siyam), Plato, asceticism, purity, transparency, Garshuni, Neoplatonism, Divine Nature</keywords>

### Prayer
To be recited in a time of spiritual distress <note>original: "al-kurh," referring to a state of internal hardship or aversion</note>, to be said to God—Mighty and Sublime: O God, O Creator of the world and its governance; You who established things that have no essence of their own. You are my Lord and I am Your servant. I ask that You look into the substance <note>original: "jawhar"</note> of my soul, which has not yet been purified. Fill me with light from Your light. I ask You, my God, by the "Alpha of Alphas" <note>likely a reference to the divine "Aleph" or a Greek-influenced philosophical term for the First Cause</note> of Your essence, through which You draw near by Your wisdom that has no end. And by the name of the Living God, from whose presence every living thing exists, may You give life to the rational soul <note>original: "al-nafs al-natiqa," a technical term in medieval philosophy for the human soul's highest faculty</note>. Grant me a sanctuary from Your sanctuary, enlightening and exalted within Your mighty essence, which lacks nothing except what is not You. For You are the Merciful, the Holy, the Sanctified, and You are the one who does not cease. Amen.

---

### Prayer
To be said after the night prayer in a time of distress: O my God, O He to whose majesty there is no end. You are the one who created the world by Your perfect essence, which is withheld from all others, and in which none but the wise perceive Your majesty. I ask You, O Creator, You who in Your essence never were and never will be anything other than Your essence, the Mighty and Sublime. There is no path into it except from Your essence, not from any other. To the Mighty Majesty belongs the work and all of humanity. Glory be to Him <note>original: "subhanahu"</note>; You are the Exalted Creator and the Holy One who shall never cease to be. Amen.

---

### Prayer
To God, by way of liberation from physical matters <note>original: "al-mawad al-jismaniya"</note>: My God, I am Your servant, indeed [a part of] Your essence. By Your might and Your mercy, You are the Mighty One whose essence cannot be confined. I have longed to look upon Your wisdom and Your mercy, because the dense matters <note>original: "al-mawad al-kathifa," referring to the heavy, physical elements of the material world</note> have pulled down my soul in this murky and dark world, and I have felt overwhelmed. I have been emptied of Your guiding light, in which the purity and majesty of Your essence are never extinguished. O God, have mercy on me and grant me a purity from Your purity, O my God. Amen.

---

### Prayer
To God at the time of drinking the beverage <note>This likely refers to a ritual cup or a metaphorical "wine" of divine knowledge</note> which is a light from the essence of God and is not a physical body: I ask You, O God, that I may succeed in Your service, and that You make me a servant to You. Let me look upon what You do through Your mighty wisdom, which never regrets a beginning and has no end. Say within your soul: Amen. May You be sustained. Amen.

### Prayer
Regarding the stripping away <note>original: "al-tajrid," a mystical term for divesting the soul of material desires or worldly attachments</note> of physical matters and for the Holy One, Glory be to Him, who dwells in Your essence which shall never cease to be. Amen.

# From here the book begins
## Introduction to the Book

Behold, before I approach—with the help of God <note>original: "be-ezrat HaShem yitbarakh," a standard invocation for divine assistance</note>—to explain the depth of goodness and the knowledge placed within the secret of the foundations of the Torah and the faith in <term>Divine Providence</term> <note>Hebrew: "Hashgachah Pratit"; the belief that God intervenes in the lives of individuals, not just universal laws</note> and the merit of the Torah. Know that in this book there are matters appropriate for every single man, for high and purifying souls, and there are simple matters here for every person according to his value and standing. They too are for the honor of the Creator, blessed be He, to understand those wondrous deeds which were done in our Torah, and to make known the kindness of the God who guides Israel at every time and season—especially the <term>Hidden Righteous</term> <note>Hebrew: "Tzadikim HaNistarim"; in Jewish mysticism, these are holy individuals whose identities remain unknown to the world, but whose merit sustains existence</note> who were hidden and whom no human eye beheld until they come before the King of Kings, the Holy One, Blessed be He, forever.

In the name of the Lord we shall act and succeed. May the pleasantness of the Lord our God be upon us; establish the work of our hands for us; yes, establish the work of our hands <note>A common liturgical citation of Psalm 90:17</note>. And to further add and deepen the thought that the world is a wheel <note>referring to the cyclical nature of fortune or reincarnation</note> and to show a portion of His holy <term>Shekhinah</term> <note>the Divine Presence or the feminine aspect of God dwelling in the world</note>. And in a simple place, to satisfy those who know Torah with wisdom, understanding, and wondrous secrets. There are fifteen parts in this book which are "good and bad" for a person who ascends in his days because of them. Each part is divided into chapters which will explain "smiling faces" <note>favorable circumstances or divine mercy</note> and "angry faces" <note>difficulties or divine judgment</note>. It is by the mercies of the Lord that we are not consumed. In His love and compassion, He had pity on man lest he be cast out from his world. He expressed His thought and said: "Let not the wise man glory in his wisdom... but let him that glories glory in this: that he understands and knows Me" <note>A translation of Jeremiah 9:22-23</note>. And behold, this scripture explains that there is no wisdom greater than knowing the Lord, His deeds, and His path, so as to resemble his Creator. Just as He is good to all people, so too shall we do, for the righteousness of the Lord stands forever and ever.

And he who desires and submits his awe, saying: "I will speak of the glorious honor of Your majesty and of Your wondrous works" <note>Psalm 145:5</note>, for then, by the light of Your face, they shall find favor and eternal love through my love for them. And I shall see that everything is within the weighing of man's intellect; within him stands evil, but with his heart he shall understand and guard, and in their hearts they shall acquire the fear of the Lord as eternal servants.

<margin>The Book of Wonders <note>Hebrew: "Sefer HaMufla'im"</note> concerning the foundations of the Lord and their thought, etc., and the wisdom and the riddle are within the secret of "Tear the Accuser" <note>Hebrew: "Karia Satan"; a mystical formula from the *Ana B'Koach* prayer intended to remove spiritual blockages</note>, etc.</margin>

May He place it in our hearts in truth and integrity, for the Name, may He be blessed, is Blessed, and His Name is One. This shall our children and our servants know: everyone who believes in the Lord, may He be blessed—He and His Name are unified by our mouths and our hearts forever and ever. These are straightforward things to those who understand, like cold water to a weary soul.

-> A Prayer for the Poor: Our God, teach us the light of life through Your knowledge, and to understand words of insight by the light of Your face. <-

<note>This is a quotation from the biblical Song of Songs 3:11. In Jewish tradition, King Solomon is the central figure of that book, and this phrase is frequently used on title pages of works attributed to or concerning Solomon.</note>

# Book of Solomon

-> A son, a crown, a tangled rose: a gift <-
<note>original: "Ben zar sharach shoshan: teshur." This is a complex poetic line. "Shoshan" (lily or rose) was a common symbol for the city of Amsterdam in Hebrew printing, often referred to as the "Lily of the Valleys."</note>
-> Quickly we come, descending to the King's garden — <-
-> With tidings and with logical premises <-
-> We shall recount his wondrous wisdom <-
-> Therein <-
-> And by his mouth, he shall kiss with the wine of the mixture <-
<note>A play on Song of Songs 1:2, "Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth," here referring to the "intoxicating" nature of wisdom or wine.</note>
-> And we shall enjoy in peace, spread out like the years <-
-> The showbread of the presence in his kingdom <-
-> And they shall be for three, for charity <-
-> Until Shiloh comes, and we <-
<note>original: "Shiloh." This is a messianic reference to Genesis 49:10, "Until Shiloh comes," expressing a hope for the future redemption while the author presents this work in the present.</note>
-> To shorten for his beautiful <term>pilpul</term> <-
<note>original: "pilpulo." Pilpul is a traditional method of Talmudic study that uses intense legal analysis and hair-splitting dialectic reasoning to harmonize conflicting texts.</note>
-> The spirit of his lips, the flow of his lips <-
-> In examination I have arranged and turned <-

-> Here in <-
-> Amsterdam, in the year <-
-> "A miracle... and the city shall flourish" <-
<note>The original text uses a chronogram—a common practice where certain letters in a Hebrew phrase are marked to be added up numerically to indicate the year. The phrase "the city shall flourish" is a standard blessing for Amsterdam in Hebrew books of this period.</note>

<summary>This is the title page of the "Book of Solomon," featuring a poetic introduction full of biblical allusions and identifying Amsterdam as the place of publication. The text uses floral and royal imagery to frame the "wondrous wisdom" contained in the manuscript.</summary>

<keywords>Book of Solomon, Amsterdam, Hebrew printing, Pilpul, Song of Songs, chronogram, Jewish scholarship</keywords>

-> Here in <-

-> Amsterdam, in the year <-

-> "A miracle... and the city shall..." <-
<note>original: likely a Hebrew chronogram based on a biblical verse such as Psalm 72:16. In this tradition, specific letters in a chosen verse are marked; their numerical values are then summed to provide the year of the book's completion or printing. The phrase here suggests a blessing for the city of Amsterdam.</note>

<summary>This page concludes the manuscript's front matter, identifying Amsterdam as the place of origin and providing a traditional dated chronogram.</summary>

<keywords>Amsterdam, chronogram, manuscript production, title page, 17th-century printing tradition</keywords>

While often used by later owners for signatures or personal notes, this specific page remained unused.</note>
